# Tool inventory — the stage-1 compatibility table

**Status: in progress.** Groups filled so far: **system and search (34)**, **readonly (34)**,
**compact (22)**, **high (156)**.
Remaining: low (116).

This is the table the design
(`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-08-result-error-strategies-design.md`) makes a
precondition for stage 3: *"No tool loses an input or an output field that is not in
it."* Every later migration plan is written from these rows.

The first five columns — tool, group, implementation files, visible in, current inputs —
are carried **verbatim** from `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-09-tool-inventory-rows.md`
(Task 1a, commit `d74a797`), which produced them from two committed scripts and had them
re-derived independently by two reviewers. They are not re-derived here. Where a row of
that document looks wrong, it is recorded under **Findings about the skeleton** below
rather than silently corrected.

The remaining eight columns come from reading each handler body — not its
`TOOL_DEFINITION` — on branch `feat/answer-adapter`, which is `main` plus the two Task 1a
commits. No handler was modified by this task.

## How to read the eight new columns

| column | what is in it |
|---|---|
| current default output | the object the caller receives today, field by field. Where the handler builds a JSON string and hands it to `return_response`, the fields listed are the fields **inside** that string; the MCP envelope is mentioned only where it deviates from `{ isError, content: [{ type: 'text', text }] }` |
| transformation today | how the ADT answer becomes that output — the parser by name, or "returns the document" where the body is passed through untouched |
| error decision today | what the handler treats as a failure: an HTTP status, a thrown error, a field in the document — and what it treats as a **success** that a reader might not expect |
| guarantees | every check, rejection or resolution the handler performs beyond passing the answer through, quoted from its own comment where it has one. A guarantee that depends on a field is a reason that field stays |
| new inputs | the inputs after `detail` is added and any removed parameter is gone. **`+detail`** is shorthand for "every current input unchanged, plus `detail`: `'terse' \| 'full' \| 'raw'`, default `'terse'`" |
| new terse projection | the fields `terse` carries. `full` carries the whole parsed structure and `raw` the document, per the design, so only `terse` needs stating |
| dropped | every field in *current default output* absent from *new terse projection* |
| justification | why each dropped field may go |

**The rule for the last two columns**, from the design: *a field the caller needs to make
the next call — an object name it did not already have, a transport number it must quote —
stays in `terse`; a field that only describes what just happened moves to `full`.*

Two consequences of that rule recur often enough to state once rather than in 362 rows:

- **`success: true` is dropped everywhere it appears.** It is the same claim the MCP
  envelope's `isError: false` already makes, and under the new adapter a value is
  reachable only through `ok` — so a payload asserting its own success is not merely
  redundant, it is the shape the three masking fixes removed.
- **A field that echoes the caller's own argument is dropped.** `class_name` in the answer
  to `GetClass(class_name: 'ZCL_X')` is not a name the caller "did not already have".
  Where an argument is *normalised* before use — uppercased, trimmed, resolved against a
  second object — the normalised value **stays**, because that one the caller did not have.

Where a row says *"returns the document"* in *transformation today*, the current default
output already is the raw body. Moving that tool to a `terse` projection is a **reduction
of the default**, and the row says so: a caller that parses the XML itself must ask for
`detail: 'raw'`.

## Masking: refusals reported as empty successes

This repository has fixed three defects of one shape — read masking, deletion masking,
activation masking — each a refusal delivered as `{ success: true }` with nothing in it.
The **error decision today** column names every further instance found, and they are
collected here as they are found, group by group.

From **system and search**:

| tool | the masking |
|---|---|
| `GetTransaction` | unconditional. The handler is a stub: it ignores `transaction_name`, makes no request, and answers `isError: false` with `{ message: 'Not implemented' }`. Its parser `_parseTransactionXml` is dead code |
| `GetSqlQuery` | `parseSqlQueryXml` catches its own parse failure and returns `{ columns: [], rows: [], error: 'Failed to parse XML response' }`; the handler returns that with `isError: false`. A document it could not read is delivered as an empty result set |
| `GetObjectNodeFromCache` | when expanding `OBJECT_URI` throws, the failure is written **into the returned node** as `object_uri_response: 'ERROR: <message>'` and the node is answered as a success |
| `SearchObject` | a 200 whose body contains no `<adtcore:objectReference ` is answered `{ isError: false, content: [] }` — an empty `content` array with no text at all. "Found nothing" is a legitimate search answer, but the same branch swallows an unparseable or unauthorised 200, and the caller cannot tell them apart |
| `GetObjectsByType` | a 200 the regex could not read yields the text `No objects found for node_id …` with `isError: false` — same conflation as `SearchObject`, in prose rather than an empty array |
| `GetAdtTypes` | a 200 that is not a `nameditem:namedItemList` yields the text `[]` with `isError: false` |
| `DescribeByList` | an object that could not be described is **dropped from the answer** by an empty `catch {}` with no marker, so a short list reads as "these are the objects". Separately, its outer catch answers `{ isError: true, content: [] }` — an error with no message |
| `GetObjectInfo` | enrichment failure is swallowed by `catch (_e) {}`, leaving `OBJECT_DESCRIPTION` and `OBJECT_PACKAGE` `undefined` on every node. Since the enrichment call is itself broken (see Findings), this is the *only* outcome |
| `RuntimeRunClass`, `RuntimeRunProgram` | `output` is `response.data` when it is a string **and the empty string when it is not** — a body the handler did not recognise becomes `output: ''` alongside `success: true` |

## Findings about the handlers

Recorded, not corrected — this task writes one document and touches no `src/`.

### `GetObjectInfo`'s `enrich` has never worked

`enrichNodeWithSearchObject` (`handleGetObjectInfo.ts:82-87`) calls

```ts
const searchResult = await handleSearchObject(context, {
  query: objectName, object_type: objectType, maxResults: 1,
});
```

`handleSearchObject` destructures `object_name` and answers
`return_error('object_name is required')` when it is absent — so every enrichment call
returns `isError: true`, the `!searchResult.isError` gate below it is false, and the loop
never runs. Even if the parameter name were fixed, the body would still fail: the function
feeds `entry.text` to an `XMLParser` and looks for `adtcore:objectReferences`, while
`handleSearchObject` returns **tab-separated text**, not XML. Two independent defects, so
`OBJECT_DESCRIPTION` and `OBJECT_PACKAGE` are always `undefined` and `enrich` costs a
round trip per node for nothing.

This matters to the migration: a plan reading only the `TOOL_DEFINITION` would preserve
`enrich` as a working feature.

### Three inputs are accepted and never read

| tool | input | evidence |
|---|---|---|
| `GetAdtTypes` | `validate_type` | the handler signature is `(context, _args)`; nothing validates a type. The description advertises "or validate a specific type name" |
| `GetPackageTree` | `debug` | declared in the schema with a description promising "diagnostic metadata in response (counts, types, hierarchy info)"; `args.debug` appears nowhere in the body |
| `GetSession` | `force_new` | read into a local and used only to decorate a debug log line; no branch depends on it |

### `GetSession` returns `session_state: null` while 100+ tools ask for it

`handleGetSession.ts:69` hardcodes `session_state: null` with the comment *"Session state
management is now handled by auth-broker"*. Every `*Low` tool's schema still describes
`session_state` as *"Session state from GetSession (cookies, csrf_token, cookie_store).
Required if session_id is provided."* The documented handshake cannot be completed with
what `GetSession` returns.

### `objectsListCache` is written in two incompatible shapes

`GetTypeInfo`, `GetWhereUsed` and `GetObjectsByType` call `objectsListCache.setCache()`
with a whole **MCP result object** (`{ isError, content }`). `GetObjectsList` calls it with
`{ parent_name, parent_tech_name, parent_type, total_objects, objects }`.
`GetObjectNodeFromCache` reads `cache.objects` — which only the second shape has. A cache
last written by any of the first three makes `GetObjectNodeFromCache` answer
`Node not found in cache` regardless of what is in it.

### Content items of type `json`, and results with no `isError`

Six handlers return `content: [{ type: 'json', json: … }]` — a content item type the MCP
content schema does not define: `GetObjectNodeFromCache`, `GetTypeInfo`, `GetWhereUsed`,
`GetObjectInfo`, `GetAbapSystemSymbols`, `GetTransaction`.

Two omit `isError` from the success result entirely: `GetObjectNodeFromCache` and
`GetObjectsList`. `GetObjectsList` additionally returns a top-level `cache` key beside
`content`, which is not part of an MCP result.

### `GetObjectInfo` is a fourth unbounded-ish traversal

The design names three traversals to bound (`GetPackageTree`, `GetPackageContents`,
`GetObjectsList`). `GetObjectInfo` also recurses — `buildTree` calls itself per group node
— but is already bounded by `maxDepth` (default 2 for `PROG/*` and `FUGR/*`, 1 otherwise).
It is not unbounded, so it needs no new bound; it is recorded so the migration does not
discover it as a surprise.

## Findings about the skeleton

None for this group. Every system and search row's implementation file, group and input
list matched the handler as read.

The alternate `object_type`/`object_name` spelling **stays accepted**: removing it is a breaking change that this table would have to carry, and it costs two `??` |

Any throw → `return_error`. The document is never inspected | requires both names. Restores the caller's session when **both** `session_id` and `session_state` are given | +detail | `{ nodes: [{ nodeid, parentid, objecttype, objectname }] }` | the rest of the document | **a reduction of the default**, same as `GetNodeStructureLow`: today the whole XML comes back. A caller that parses it passes `detail: 'raw'`. Note this tool and `GetObjectStructure` read the same endpoint and differ only in whether the answer is parsed — after the migration they differ only in the group they live in, which is a candidate for consolidation but not this table's decision |

The pre-flight read returning falsy, or throwing a 404 → `return_error(\`Package ${packageName} not found\`)`; any other throw from the pre-flight is re-thrown. A falsy hierarchy → `return_error(\`Failed to fetch package tree for ${packageName}\`)`. Any throw → `return_error` | **quoted from the handler: `// Verify package exists before building tree (fixes #38)`** — it reads the package first and answers `Package X not found` rather than walking a name that does not exist and returning an empty tree. That is a guarantee of exactly the shape this migration protects, and it must survive. Also uppercases `package_name`; treats `include_subpackages` and `include_descriptions` as true unless explicitly `false`; defaults `max_depth` to 5 | package_name*, **depth**, include_descriptions, +detail. `include_subpackages` and `max_depth` **removed** — refused by name per the design, `depth: 1` = the old `include_subpackages: false`, `depth: 5` = the old default. `debug` **removed** — refused by name, `message: "debug was never implemented"` | `{ package_name, tree, stopped_at_bound, not_expanded: [package_name] }` | `metadata` in full — `include_subpackages`, `max_depth`, `include_descriptions` | every field of `metadata` echoes the caller's own arguments, and two of the three no longer exist. `package_name` **stays** — it is uppercased before use, so it is not the string the caller passed. `stopped_at_bound` and `not_expanded` are **added** per the design: resuming is by package name, which the caller can call this same tool with. **Default behaviour changes**: five levels today, as deep as the bound allows after |

In practice unreachable — nothing in the body can fail | none. `force_new` is **accepted and never acted on** (see Findings) | **`force_new` removed** — refused by name, `message: "force_new was never implemented; GetSession always mints a new id"`. +detail. Every lookup exhausted → `{ isError:true, content:[{type:'text', text:'Type X was not found as domain, data element, table type, or structure.'}] }`. A non-404 throw **aborts the chain** and reaches `return_error` | two, and both matter. **`hasUsableResult`** refuses an empty body, an empty parse, or a `{ raw: {} }` as a hit, so an empty 200 falls through to the next lookup instead of being answered as the type. **`isNotFoundError`** restricts that fall-through to HTTP 404 — any other failure aborts rather than being retried as "not found", so an authorisation failure on the first URL is not reported as "type not found". Translates 406 into a message naming `object_type` as the likely cause, which is a real diagnosis a caller cannot make from the status alone. **Two other handlers parse this output** — `DescribeByList` (correctly) and `GetObjectInfo` (broken, see Findings) — so its shape is an internal contract, not only a tool answer | object_name*, object_type, maxResults, +detail | `{ results: [{ name, type, description, package }] }` as JSON | the TSV rendering — the header line and the tab separators | the four values are the data; TSV is one rendering, and it forces every consumer to split on tabs and re-guess types. `description` and `package` **stay**: a caller choosing between two hits needs both, and `package` is required for any subsequent create. The empty-content branch must become either an explicit empty `results` array or a refusal — never `content: []` |
| SearchSource | search | `src/handlers/system/readonly/handleSearchSource.ts` | readonly, high, low, readonly,high, readonly,low, compact | query*, query2, exclude, packages*, include_subpackages, object_filter, object_types, exclude_comments, max_hits_per_object, emit_no_hits, max_objects, concurrency, version, time_budget_ms | `{ results: [{ devclass, object_type, object_name, include, line, snippet }], no_hits?: [{ devclass, object_type, object_name }], scanned: { packages, objects, sources }, truncated: { by_object_cap, by_max_objects, by_timeout } }` as an indented JSON string | none in the handler — `runSearchSourceWithContext` (`src/lib/search-source/orchestrator.ts`) resolves package masks, enumerates targets and scans sources, and returns the structure above | any throw → `return_error`. **Truncation is not an error**: a capped or timed-out scan answers `isError: false` with the `truncated` flags set. This is the one tool in the repository that already implements the design's "a bounded answer says so" rule | the input schema is **zod**, not JSON Schema, and enforces bounds the other tools only document: `concurrency` at most 16, `exclude` at most 3 entries, `packages` at least 1, `query` non-empty, `max_hits_per_object`/`max_objects`/`time_budget_ms` positive integers. The orchestrator returns a **partial** answer with `by_timeout: true` on deadline rather than cutting the caller off. Results are sorted deterministically (devclass, type, name, include, line) | all current inputs unchanged, +detail | `{ results, truncated, no_hits? }` | `scanned` | `truncated` **stays in `terse` and is not negotiable** — it is how the caller learns the answer is partial, and the design ranks that above brevity. `no_hits` is already opt-in via `emit_no_hits` and stays where the caller asked for it. `scanned` is three counters describing the scan that just ran; it belongs to `full`. **`available_in: ['onprem','legacy']`** — cloud has no indexed source-search endpoint |

## Findings — readonly

### `GetEnhancements` ignores a required input and reads five undeclared ones

Its schema requires `object_name` **and `object_type`**. The handler never reads
`object_type`: `determineObjectTypeAndPath` decides the type itself by probing
`/oo/classes/{n}`, then `/programs/programs/{n}`, then `/programs/includes/{n}` and taking
the first that answers 200. So a caller must pass a value that changes nothing, and a
caller who passes the *wrong* value is not corrected — it was never consulted.

In the other direction the handler reads five keys the schema does not declare, each of
which changes behaviour: `args.program` (manual parent-program context for an include),
`args.include_nested` (recurse into every include), `args.detailed` (full output rather
than the filtered one), `args.timeout` (per-request timeout, default 30000) and
`args.max_includes` (cap on includes processed, default 50). Depending on how the MCP
layer treats unknown properties, these are either unreachable or reachable-but-undocumented;
either way the tool's public surface and its behaviour disagree.

`GetIncludesList` has a smaller version of the same defect: `detailed` is declared —
*"If true, returns structured JSON with metadata and raw XML"* — and never read.

### Sixteen `Read*` handlers answer `{ success: true, source_code: null }`

`ReadBehaviorDefinition`, `ReadBehaviorImplementation`, `ReadClass`, `ReadDataElement`,
`ReadDdl`, `ReadDomain`, `ReadFunctionGroup`, `ReadFunctionInclude`, `ReadInterface`,
`ReadMetadataExtension`, `ReadPackage`, `ReadServiceBinding`, `ReadServiceDefinition`,
`ReadStructure`, `ReadTable` — and `ReadMessageClass` / `ReadMessageClassMessage` in their
own shape — all follow one template:

```ts
let source_code: string | null = null;
const readResult = await obj.read(…);
if (readResult?.readResult?.data) { source_code = …; }
…
return return_response({ data: JSON.stringify({ success: true, …, source_code, metadata }) });
```

A read that produced no `data` is therefore delivered as `success: true` with
`source_code: null`. That is the **read-masking** defect this repository already fixed once,
still present in sixteen tools. `ReadProgram` is the exception and shows what the fix looks
like: it inspects the metadata and answers `success: false, error: 'invalid_object_type'`
rather than a null-bearing success — and its own comment says it is doing exactly that.

This is the single largest reason the readonly group is worth migrating first.

### `GetEnhancementImpl` answers "not found" as a success

When the enhancement URL does not return 200-with-data, the handler makes a **second**
request for the spot and answers `isError: false` with
`{ status: 'not_found', message: …, spot_metadata }`. Unlike the sixteen above it names the
condition in a field, so a caller *can* branch — but it is still a refusal delivered as a
success, and the field is not one any other tool uses.

### Two traversals in this group already report their own bounds

`GetIncludesList` walks the include tree to `MAX_INCLUDE_DEPTH = 20`, marking a node
`truncated: true` at the limit and `cyclic: true` when it revisits a name.
`GetStructuresList` walks embeds/appends with a `visited` set, marks `cyclic`, records
`error` on a node whose source it could not read, and sets `appends_unavailable: true` when
the where-used lookup failed — its comment: *"Do NOT swallow the failure into an empty
result — flag the response as degraded so callers can tell 'could not determine appends'
apart from 'no appends'."* Both are the pattern the design asks the three package/node
traversals to adopt, already implemented here.

### Findings about the skeleton

None for this group. Every readonly row's implementation file, group and input list matched
the handler as read. Note that the skeleton's *current inputs* for `GetEnhancements` is
`object_name*, object_type*`, which is correct — it reports the schema, and the schema is
what the finding above is about.

## Findings — compact

### The five "dead" handlers the skeleton found are not dead code

Task 1a recorded five files under `src/handlers/common/low/` whose `TOOL_DEFINITION` no
group registers — `handleCheckObject`, `handleDeleteObject`, `handleLockObject`,
`handleUnlockObject`, `handleValidateObject` — and concluded they are "dead, deliberately
disabled handlers". That is right about the **tools** and wrong about four of the five
**handler functions**: the compact group calls them directly.

| unregistered file | called by |
|---|---|
| `handleActivateObject` (registered as `ActivateObjectLow`) | `HandlerActivate` |
| `handleCheckObject` | `HandlerCheckRun` |
| `handleLockObject` | `HandlerLock` |
| `handleUnlockObject` | `HandlerUnlock` |
| `handleValidateObject` | `HandlerValidate`, and `handleDeleteObject` — genuinely unused |

So `handleDeleteObject` is the only one of the five that nothing reaches. The other four are
the implementation of four compact tools, are on the live path in the `compact` exposition,
and must be migrated with it. A migration plan acting on Task 1a's wording alone would have
deleted them.

### The compact group is 22 delegations and almost no logic of its own

Every compact tool is a thin adapter over one or more `high`, `low` or `system` handlers.
Its **output is its delegate's output, unchanged** — no compact handler reshapes a result.
What each one does add is:

- a **name mapping**, `CompactObjectType` (`CLASS`, `PROGRAM`, …) → the delegate's own
  vocabulary, via `compactRouterMap` for CRUD and `toLowObjectType` for the lifecycle four;
- a **refusal** when the pair is not in the map.

That has one consequence the migration must not miss: **`terse` for a compact tool is the
delegate's `terse`.** Twenty-two rows below therefore say "as `<delegate>`", and the
projection decision lives in the delegate's row, not here. Writing a second, independent
projection for the compact surface would be how the two drift apart.

### Compact refusals are thrown, not returned

`HandlerActivate`, `HandlerCheckRun`, `HandlerLock`, `HandlerUnlock`, `HandlerValidate` and
`HandlerProfileRun` raise a bare `throw new Error(...)` for an unsupported `object_type` or
a missing conditional argument, rather than answering `return_error`. These do reach the
caller as errors — `return_error`'s own comment confirms *"both registration paths route any
thrown error through here"* — but they take the handler's own error shaping out of the loop.
Under the new design a `throw` inside a handler is not covered by `answer`'s boundary either,
since it happens before the call; each of these six needs an explicit refusal.

### `compactRouterMap` is checked against `COMPACT_CRUD_MATRIX` at module load

`validateCompactRouterAgainstMatrix()` runs at import time and **throws on any mismatch**,
so a router entry added without a matrix entry (or the reverse) fails the process at startup
rather than producing an "Unsupported operation" at call time. This is a guarantee about the
tool surface itself and it is cheap to lose in a refactor.

### Three shared parsers mask a refusal, and the compact group inherits all three

Named here because the compact group is where all three meet, though they belong to
`src/lib/`:

| parser | the masking |
|---|---|
| `parseCheckRunResponse` (`src/lib/checkRunParser.ts:50`) | a document with no `chkrun:checkReport` returns `{ success: true, status: 'no_report', message: 'No check report in response (no issues reported)' }`. A body it could not read is reported as "no issues" |
| `parseValidationResponse` (`src/lib/utils.ts:1073`) | *"No data means validation passed"* — an `asx:abap/asx:values/DATA` it cannot find yields `{ valid: true }` |
| `parseActivationResponse` (`src/lib/utils.ts:2185`) | the one that does **not** mask: a parse failure returns `activated: false` with an `error` message. It is the model the other two need |

`handleActivateObject` then computes `success = activated && checked` and answers through
`return_response` **regardless** — so a failed activation reaches the caller as `isError:
false` carrying `success: false`. That is the activation-masking shape, on the
`activateObjectsGroup` path rather than the `activateObjectInSession` path fixed in 8.10.0.

### Findings about the skeleton

None for this group. The 22 compact rows' files, groups and input lists matched.

## Table — compact (22 rows)

| tool | group | implementation files | visible in | current inputs | current default output | transformation today | error decision today | guarantees | new inputs | new terse projection | dropped | justification |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| HandlerActivate | compact | `src/handlers/compact/high/handleHandlerActivate.ts` | compact | object_type, object_name, object_adt_type, objects, preaudit | `handleActivateObject`'s: `{ success, objects_count, objects: [{ name, uri, type }], activation: { activated, checked, generated }, messages: [{ type, text, line?, column? }], warnings, errors, message }` as an indented JSON string, **always with `isError: false`** | none of its own — it normalises single-object arguments into a one-element `objects` array and calls `handleActivateObject`, which uses `parseActivationResponse` | neither `objects` nor the `object_name` + `object_adt_type` pair → **`throw new Error('Provide either objects[] or object_name + object_adt_type for activation')`**. Everything else is the delegate's: an empty/non-array `objects` → `return_error`; a throw during activation → `return_error` with the response body appended. **A failed activation is not a failure** — see Findings | accepts a single object *or* a batch and normalises to one shape, so the caller does not build the array. The delegate uppercases every object name and defaults `preaudit` to true | object_type, object_name, object_adt_type, objects, preaudit, +detail | `{ activated, messages, errors }` — as `ActivateObjectLow` | `success`, `objects_count`, `objects`, `activation.checked`, `activation.generated`, `warnings`, `message` | as `ActivateObjectLow`'s row in the `low` group, which is where the decision lives. The one addition here: `success = activated && checked` **must become the `isError` of the answer**, not a field — that is the masking fix, and the compact surface is where a caller most often sees it |

The delegate uses `parseCheckRunResponse` | an object type `toLowObjectType` does not map → **`throw new Error('CheckRun is not supported for object_type: X')`**. The delegate then rejects a type outside its own eleven-entry `validTypes` list with `return_error`, rejects a missing name, and turns a check that returned no response into a throw. **`parseCheckRunResponse` masking applies** — a body with no check report answers `status: 'no_report'` and `success: true` | the delegate validates `object_type` against an explicit list **and** `version` against `['active','inactive']`, falling back to `'active'` rather than passing an unknown value on. ## Findings — high

### One rule this group needs before its rows can be read

The design fixes `terse` for a **write** at `SUCCESS`. That overrides the "a normalised
argument stays" convention stated at the top for this group: an uppercased echo of the name
the caller itself passed is not worth defeating the design's one explicit `terse` rule.
Where a write produces a value the caller **did not** have — a transport number, a `run_id`,
a service name SAP filled in, a function group resolved from metadata — that value stays.
Every write row below follows exactly that, and says which case it is.

### Two tools exist only to refuse, and that is correct

`DeleteUnitTest` and `UpdateUnitTest` make **no client call at all**. Each validates
`run_id` and then answers `return_error` with `Cannot delete/update unit test run <id>: ADT
does not support deleting/updating test runs.` This is the right shape — a caller learns the
operation does not exist rather than discovering it from a 404 — and it is the exact mirror
of `GetTransaction` in the system group, which answers a success it cannot make good on.
Both should gain `code: 'UNSUPPORTED_OPERATION'` when migrated so a caller can branch
without matching prose, and both are candidates for removal from the surface, since a tool
that can only fail still costs a schema slot in every listing.

### `CreateFunctionGroup` returns a success it has not verified

`src/handlers/function/high/handleCreateFunctionGroup.ts:311-338`. On HTTP 400 whose body or
message mentions **"Kerberos library not loaded"** or **"Business partner"**, the handler
answers `success: true` with the message *"Function group X may have been created
successfully (SAP returned error but object exists)"*. Its own comment is explicit:

```
// Check if object was actually created by trying to get it
// For now, we'll assume it was created and return success
// This is a known issue with FunctionGroup creation in SAP
```

This is the masking defect written down and shipped. `CreatePackage` shows what the same
situation looks like when done properly: on HTTP 404 with *"Error while importing object"* it
**reads the package back** and answers success only if the read succeeded, adding
`warning: 'Import warning during create (404). Object verified by read.'`. The
`CreateFunctionGroup` branch must not be ported as-is — either it verifies, or the error
reaches the caller.

### `CreateClass` returns every field twice, and reports its own errors as a success

`handleCreateClass` builds `{ success: true, data: { … }, …the same five fields again… }` —
`class_name`, `package_name`, `transport_request`, `activated` and `errors` appear both
inside `data` and beside it. No other handler does this, and no caller can know which copy is
authoritative.

Separately, when `state.errors` is non-empty the answer is still `success: true`, with the
errors in the payload and `(with N error(s))` appended to the message. `CreateCdsUnitTest`
and `UpdateCdsUnitTest` have the same shape through `test_class_state.errors`. This is the
create-side of the masking family: three fixes have closed read, deletion and activation, and
this is the fourth face of the same defect.

### `activate` defaults disagree across the group, invisibly

| defaults to `true` | defaults to `false` |
|---|---|
| `UpdateBehaviorDefinition`, `UpdateBehaviorImplementation`, `UpdateInterface`, `UpdateServiceDefinition`, `UpdateStructure`, `UpdateTable`, `UpdateDomain`, `UpdateDataElement`, `UpdateMetadataExtension`, `CreateBehaviorDefinition`, `CreateMetadataExtension`, `CreateDomain`, `CreateDataElement`, `CreateFunctionGroup`, `CreateServiceBinding` | `UpdateClass`, `UpdateProgram`, `UpdateDdl`, `UpdateFunctionModule`, `UpdateFunctionInclude`, and every `Update<X>Local*` via `activate_on_update` |

`UpdateClass` and `UpdateInterface` are the two most similar tools in the repository and
they disagree: `args.activate === true` versus `activate !== false`. The schemas describe
neither default. Nothing in this table changes it — but a migration touching all of these
files at once is the moment the divergence either gets settled or gets frozen for good.

### The high group's own guarantees, beyond the brief's two

Found by reading the bodies, and each is a reason a field or a round trip stays:

- **`Get<X>` refuses an empty read; `Read<X>` does not.** Every one of the fourteen
  `Get<X>` handlers opens with `if (!readResult || !readResult.readResult) throw new
  Error('<X> not found')`. The sixteen `readonly` `Read<X>` handlers have no such check and
  answer `success: true, source_code: null`. Two families, one operation, opposite
  behaviour on the same failure.
- **`GetFunctionModule` carries the same `containerRef` ownership check as
  `ReadFunctionModule`** — the brief named only the readonly one. Metadata is read first,
  an absent `containerRef` is a refusal, and the source is fetched with the group the
  metadata named, not the caller's.
- **`UpdateClass`, `UpdateProgram`, `UpdateDdl`, `UpdateInterface`, `UpdateStructure` and
  `UpdateTable` check the new code before writing it**, and abort if the check fails — so an
  object is never overwritten with source that does not compile. The check is skipped when
  `activate` is false, which each of them logs.
- **`UpdateFunctionGroup` reads the current metadata inside the same locked session before
  writing**, so the update cannot drop a field it did not know about.
- **`UpdateFunctionInclude` and `UpdateFunctionModule` translate HTTP 400 with no
  `transport_request` into "the object may be assigned to a transport request; pass
  `transport_request` explicitly"** — naming the one argument the caller probably omitted.
- **`CreatePackage` separates 401 from 403** — reauthenticate versus ask for a role. It is
  the only handler in the repository that does.
- **`GetLocalDefinitions` and `GetLocalTypes` put the failing URL and the first 800
  characters of the body into the HTTP 406 message**, because those includes are not
  readable on every system and a bare 406 would read as a bug in this server.
- **`CreateServiceDefinition` calls `update()` after `create()`** when `source_code` was
  given — the create-shell/update-body invariant, and the fix for the empty-body defect
  closed in core 8.7.1.
- **`CreateMessageClass` deliberately does not activate**, and says why in its header:
  message classes are not activated; individual messages are added afterwards.
- **The cleanup unlocks in `CreateDomain`, `CreateDataElement`, `UpdateDomain` and
  `UpdateDataElement` swallow their own error on purpose**, so the first failure is the one
  reported rather than being masked by a failure to clean up after it.
- **`buildObjectVersionTools`' `VERSIONED_TYPES` table excludes types with no version
  history** — quoted: *"their getVersions/getVersionSource throw 'not supported', so
  per-type version tools for them would always error"* — and copies each row's
  `available_in` from that type's own `Get<X>`, so the two cannot diverge.

### Two tools, one behaviour: `RunUnitTest` and `CreateUnitTest`

`src/handlers/unit_test/high/handleRunUnitTest.ts` and
`src/handlers/unit_test/high/handleCreateUnitTest.ts` are the same handler with two names:
identical inputs, identical uppercasing, identical `run_id`-or-throw check, identical
payload down to the message text. Both are registered. Every later plan will implement both.
Worth resolving before the migration doubles the work rather than after.

### Findings about the skeleton

None for this group. All 156 rows' files, groups and input lists matched the handlers as
read, including the fourteen `Activate*` renames resolved by hand in Task 1a — each of those
rows' second file is indeed the `low` handler the `high` group re-registers under a shorter
name, and the handler body is shared with the `*Low` row verbatim.

## Table — high (156 rows)

| tool | group | implementation files | visible in | current inputs | current default output | transformation today | error decision today | guarantees | new inputs | new terse projection | dropped | justification |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ActivateBehaviorDefinition | high | `src/lib/handlers/groups/HighLevelHandlersGroup.ts`, `src/handlers/behavior_definition/low/handleActivateBehaviorDefinition.ts` | high, readonly,high | name*, session_id, session_state | `{ success, name, activation: { activated, checked, generated }, messages: [{ type, text, line?, column? }], warnings, errors, session_id, session_state: null, message }` as an indented JSON string. `success = activated && checked`, and **the envelope is `isError: false` either way** | `parseActivationResponse` (`src/lib/utils.ts:2139`) — `fast-xml-parser` over `chkl:messages`, reading `@_activationExecuted`, `@_checkExecuted`, `@_generationExecuted` and each `msg`'s `@_type` and `shortText` | missing `name` → `return_error`. An activation that returned no response → `throw` → the inner catch. The inner catch rewrites HTTP 404 to `BehaviorDefinition {name} not found.` and otherwise pulls `exc:exception/message` out of the body with a second `fast-xml-parser` pass, then `return_error`. **A refused activation is not a failure** — `success: false` travels inside `isError: false`. See the activation-masking note in Findings | uppercases `name`. Restores the caller's session when **both** `session_id` and `session_state` are given. Splits `messages` into `warnings` and `errors` accepting **both** spellings SAP uses — `'warning'`/`'W'` and `'error'`/`'E'` | name*, session_id, session_state, +detail | `SUCCESS` when the document carried no messages; otherwise `{ messages }` — every `W` and `I` message, since an `E` makes the call a failure and its messages travel in `getError().messages` | `success`, `service_definition_name`, `activation.activated`, `activation.checked`, `activation.generated`, `warnings`, `errors`, `session_id`, `session_state`, `message` | `success` **becomes `isError`** — that is the masking fix, not a projection choice. `activated` and `checked` are what decided it, so they belong with the verdict rather than beside it; `generated` describes what just happened. `warnings` and `errors` are `messages` re-filtered, and the split is the error strategy's job once an `E` is present. `session_state` is the constant `null`; `session_id` is an echo (this handler never mints one); `message` is prose |
| ActivateStructure | high | `src/lib/handlers/groups/HighLevelHandlersGroup.ts`, `src/handlers/structure/low/handleActivateStructure.ts` | high, readonly,high | structure_name*, session_id, session_state | `{ success, structure_name, activation: { activated, checked, generated }, messages: [{ type, text, line?, column? }], warnings, errors, session_id, session_state: null, message }` as an indented JSON string. `success = activated && checked`, and **the envelope is `isError: false` either way** | `parseActivationResponse` (`src/lib/utils.ts:2139`) — `fast-xml-parser` over `chkl:messages`, reading `@_activationExecuted`, `@_checkExecuted`, `@_generationExecuted` and each `msg`'s `@_type` and `shortText` | missing `structure_name` → `return_error`. An activation that returned no response → `throw` → the inner catch. The inner catch rewrites HTTP 404 to `Structure {name} not found.` and otherwise pulls `exc:exception/message` out of the body with a second `fast-xml-parser` pass, then `return_error`. **A refused activation is not a failure** — `success: false` travels inside `isError: false`.
